.mbox-text-span{margin-left:23px!important}@media(min-width:720px){.mw-parser-output .ambox{margin:0 10%}}</style><table class="box-Expand_section plainlinks metadata ambox mbox-small-left ambox-content" role="presentation"><tbody><tr><td class="mbox-image"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Wiki_letter_w_cropped.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="[icon]" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1c/Wiki_letter_w_cropped.svg/20px-Wiki_letter_w_cropped.svg.png" decoding="async" width="20" height="14" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1c/Wiki_letter_w_cropped.svg/30px-Wiki_letter_w_cropped.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1c/Wiki_letter_w_cropped.svg/40px-Wiki_letter_w_cropped.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="44" data-file-height="31" /></a></span></td><td class="mbox-text"><div class="mbox-text-span">This section <b>needs expansion</b>. You can help by <a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Stanley_Baldwin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=">adding to it</a>.  <span class="date-container"><i>(<span class="date">March 2022</span>)</i></span></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Second_Baldwin_ministry" title="Second Baldwin ministry">Second Baldwin ministry</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:KingBaldwin1926.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d5/KingBaldwin1926.jpg/220px-KingBaldwin1926.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="302" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d5/KingBaldwin1926.jpg/330px-KingBaldwin1926.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/d/d5/KingBaldwin1926.jpg 2x" data-file-width="350" data-file-height="480" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King" title="William Lyon Mackenzie King">W. L. Mackenzie King</a>, Prime Minister of Canada (left) and Baldwin at the <a href="/wiki/Imperial_Conference" title="Imperial Conference">Imperial Conference</a>, October 1926</figcaption></figure>
<p>Baldwin's new Cabinet now included many former political associates of Lloyd George: former Coalition Conservatives: <a href="/wiki/Austen_Chamberlain" title="Austen Chamberlain">Austen Chamberlain</a> (as foreign secretary), <a href="/wiki/F_E_Smith" class="mw-redirect" title="F E Smith">Lord Birkenhead</a> (secretary for India) and Arthur Balfour (lord president after 1925), and the former Liberal <a href="/wiki/Winston_Churchill" title="Winston Churchill">Winston Churchill</a> as chancellor of the exchequer. Baldwin created the <a href="/wiki/Organisation_for_the_Maintenance_of_Supplies" title="Organisation for the Maintenance of Supplies">Organisation for the Maintenance of Supplies</a>, a volunteer body of those opposed to the strike which was intended to complete essential work.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Financial_issues">Financial issues</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Stanley_Baldwin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Financial issues">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="1926_general_strike">1926 general strike</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Stanley_Baldwin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: 1926 general strike">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>A defining feature of Baldwin's Second term was the <a href="/wiki/1926_United_Kingdom_general_strike" title="1926 United Kingdom general strike">1926 General Strike</a>, Baldwin handled the strike by using powers awarded to him in the <a href="/wiki/Emergency_Powers_Act_1920" title="Emergency Powers Act 1920">Emergency Powers Act of 1920</a>. He deployed the military and volunteers to keep essential services running. The strike ended when it was found to not be protected by the <a href="/wiki/Trade_Disputes_Act_1906" title="Trade Disputes Act 1906">Trade Disputes Act of 1906</a>, leading to the strike being called of on the 12th of May lasting just 9 days. Baldwin's government was widely credited for such an effective response to the strike.<a rel="nofollow" class="external autonumber" href="https://www.cambridge.org/core/services/aop-cambridge-core/content/view/A924DA8E7E6BBDCEF26976C74B5C7199/S0020859000003497a.pdf/the-government-and-the-general-strike-1926.pdf">[1]</a>
</p>
<figure class="mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:KingRoyals1927.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/83/KingRoyals1927.jpg/250px-KingRoyals1927.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="184" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/83/KingRoyals1927.jpg/375px-KingRoyals1927.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/83/KingRoyals1927.jpg/500px-KingRoyals1927.jpg 2x" data-file-width="760" data-file-height="559" /></a><figcaption>Visit of the <a href="/wiki/Edward_VIII" title="Edward VIII">Prince of Wales</a> (the future Edward VIII) to <a href="/wiki/Rideau_Hall" title="Rideau Hall">Rideau Hall</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ottawa" title="Ottawa">Ottawa</a> August 1927. Front row: left-right: 2nd, <a href="/wiki/William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King" title="William Lyon Mackenzie King">W. L. Mackenzie King</a>; 4th, <a href="/wiki/Freeman_Freeman-Thomas,_1st_Marquess_of_Willingdon" title="Freeman Freeman-Thomas, 1st Marquess of Willingdon">Viscount Willingdon</a>; 5th, the Prince of Wales; 6th, <a href="/wiki/Prince_George,_Duke_of_Kent" title="Prince George, Duke of Kent">Prince George</a>; 8th, Baldwin</figcaption></figure>
<p>At Baldwin's instigation <a href="/wiki/William_Weir,_1st_Viscount_Weir" title="William Weir, 1st Viscount Weir">Lord Weir</a> headed a <a href="/wiki/Committee" title="Committee">committee</a> to "review the national problem of electrical energy". It published its report on 14 May 1925 and in it Weir recommended the setting up of a <a href="/wiki/Central_Electricity_Board" title="Central Electricity Board">Central Electricity Board</a>, a state monopoly half-financed by the <a href="/wiki/British_Government" class="mw-redirect" title="British Government">Government</a> and half by local undertakings. Baldwin accepted Weir's recommendations and they became law by the end of 1926.<sup id="cite_ref-Middlemas_and_Barnes,_pp._393–4_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Middlemas_and_Barnes,_pp._393–4-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Board was a success. By 1939 electrical output was up fourfold and generating costs had fallen. Consumers of electricity rose from three-quarters of a million in 1920 to nine million in 1938, with annual growth of 700,000 to 800,000 a year (the fastest rate of growth in the world).<sup id="cite_ref-Middlemas_and_Barnes,_pp._393–4_30-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Middlemas_and_Barnes,_pp._393–4-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Legislation">Legislation</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Stanley_Baldwin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Legislation">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>One of his legislative reforms was a paradigm shift in his party. This was the Widows, Orphans and Old Age Contributory Pensions Act of 1925, which provided a pension of 10 shillings a week for widows with extra for children, and 10 shillings a week for insured workers and their wives at 65. This transformed <a href="/wiki/Toryism" class="mw-redirect" title="Toryism">Toryism</a>, away from its historic reliance on community (particularly religious) charities, and towards acceptance of a humanitarian <a href="/wiki/Welfare_state" title="Welfare state">welfare state</a> which would guarantee a minimum living standard for those unable to work or who took out <a href="/wiki/National_insurance" class="mw-redirect" title="National insurance">national insurance</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> In 1927, he was made a Fellow of the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Society" title="Royal Society">Royal Society</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Ousting_and_return_to_opposition">Ousting and return to opposition</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Stanley_Baldwin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Ousting and return to opposition">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In <a href="/wiki/1929_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1929 United Kingdom general election">1929</a> Labour returned to office as the largest party in the House of Commons (although without an overall majority) despite obtaining fewer votes than the Conservatives.<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> In opposition, Baldwin was almost ousted as party leader by the <a href="/wiki/Press_baron" class="mw-redirect" title="Press baron">press barons</a> Lords Rothermere and Beaverbrook, whom he accused of enjoying "power without responsibility, the prerogative of the harlot throughout the ages".<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Ramsden argues that Baldwin made dramatic permanent improvements to the organisation and effectiveness of the Conservative Party. He enlarged the headquarters with professionals, professionalised the party agents, raised ample funds, and was an innovative user of the new mass media of radio and film.<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Lord_President_of_the_Council_.281931.E2.80.931935.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Lord_President_of_the_Council_(1931–1935)">Lord President of the Council (1931–1935)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Stanley_Baldwin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Lord President of the Council (1931–1935)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>By 1931, as the economy headed towards crisis, both in Britain and around the world, with the onset of the <a href="/wiki/Great_Depression" title="Great Depression">Great Depression</a>, Baldwin and the Conservatives entered into a coalition with Labour Prime Minister Ramsay MacDonald.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> This decision led to MacDonald's expulsion from his own party, and Baldwin, as <a href="/wiki/Lord_President_of_the_Council" title="Lord President of the Council">Lord President of the Council</a>, became <i><a href="/wiki/De_facto" title="De facto">de facto</a></i> prime minister, deputising for the increasingly senile MacDonald, until he once again officially became prime minister in 1935.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (August 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>One central and vitally important agreement was the <a href="/wiki/Statute_of_Westminster_1931" title="Statute of Westminster 1931">Statute of Westminster 1931</a>, which conferred full self-government upon the Dominions <a href="/wiki/Canada" title="Canada">Canada</a>, <a href="/wiki/South_Africa" title="South Africa">South Africa</a>, <a href="/wiki/Australia" title="Australia">Australia</a>, <a href="/wiki/The_Irish_Free_State" class="mw-redirect" title="The Irish Free State">The Irish Free State</a> and <a href="/wiki/New_Zealand" title="New Zealand">New Zealand</a>, while preparing the first steps towards the eventual <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations" title="Commonwealth of Nations">Commonwealth of Nations</a>, and away from the designation 'British Empire'.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated1973_37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated1973-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> In 1930, the first <a href="/wiki/British_Empire_Games" class="mw-redirect" title="British Empire Games">British Empire Games</a> sports competition was held successfully among Empire nations in <a href="/wiki/Hamilton,_Ontario" title="Hamilton, Ontario">Hamilton, Ontario</a>, Canada.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (August 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>His government then secured with great difficulty the passage of the landmark <a href="/wiki/Government_of_India_Act_1935" title="Government of India Act 1935">Government of India Act 1935</a>, in the teeth of opposition from Winston Churchill, spokesman for the die-hard imperialists who filled the Conservative ranks.<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Disarmament">Disarmament</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Stanley_Baldwin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Disarmament">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Baldwin did not advocate total disarmament but believed that, as <a href="/wiki/Edward_Grey,_1st_Viscount_Grey_of_Fallodon" title="Edward Grey, 1st Viscount Grey of Fallodon">Lord Grey of Falloden</a> had stated in 1925, "great armaments lead inevitably to war".<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> However he came to believe that, as he put it on 10 November 1932: "the time has now come to an end when Great Britain can proceed with unilateral disarmament".<sup id="cite_ref-Middlemas1_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Middlemas1-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> On 10 November 1932 Baldwin said:
</p>
<blockquote><p>I think it is well also for the man in the street to realise that there is no power on earth that can protect him from being bombed. Whatever people may tell him, <a href="/wiki/The_bomber_will_always_get_through" title="The bomber will always get through">the bomber will always get through</a>, The only defence is in offence, which means that you have to kill more women and children more quickly than the enemy if you want to save yourselves...If the conscience of the young men should ever come to feel, with regard to this one instrument [bombing] that it is evil and should go, the thing will be done; but if they do not feel like that – well, as I say, the future is in their hands. But when the next war comes, and European civilisation is wiped out, as it will be, and by no force more than that force, then do not let them lay blame on the old men. Let them remember that they, principally, or they alone, are responsible for the terrors that have fallen upon the earth.<sup id="cite_ref-Middlemas1_40-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Middlemas1-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>This speech was often used against Baldwin as allegedly demonstrating the futility of rearmament or disarmament, depending on the critic.<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>With the second part of the Disarmament Conference starting in January 1933, Baldwin attempted to see through his hope of air disarmament.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> However he became alarmed at Britain's lack of defence against air raids and German rearmament, saying it "would be a terrible thing, in fact, the beginning of the end".<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> In April 1933 the Cabinet agreed to follow through with the construction of the <a href="/wiki/Singapore" title="Singapore">Singapore</a> military base.<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 15 September 1933 the German delegate at the Disarmament Conference refused to return to the Conference and Germany left altogether in October. On 6 October Baldwin, in a speech to the Conservative Party Conference in Birmingham, pleaded for a Disarmament Convention and then said:
</p>
<blockquote><p>when I speak of a Disarmament Convention I do not mean disarmament on the part of this country and not on the part of any other. I mean the limitation of armaments as a real limitation...and if we find ourselves on some lower rating and that some other country has higher figures, that country has to come down and we have to go up until we meet.<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>On 14 October Germany left the <a href="/wiki/League_of_Nations" title="League of Nations">League of Nations</a>. The Cabinet decided on 23 October that Britain should still attempt to cooperate with other states, including Germany, in international disarmament.<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> However between mid-September 1933 and the beginning of 1934 Baldwin's mind changed from hoping for disarmament to favouring rearmament, including parity in aircraft.<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> In late 1933 and early 1934 he rejected an invitation from Hitler to meet him, believing that visits to foreign capitals were the job of Foreign Secretaries.<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> On 8 March 1934 Baldwin defended the creation of four new squadrons for the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Air_Force" title="Royal Air Force">Royal Air Force</a> against Labour criticisms and said of international disarmament:
</p>
<blockquote><p>If all our efforts for an agreement fail, and if it is not possible to obtain this equality in such matters as I have indicated, then any Government of this country—a National Government more than any, and <i>this</i> Government—will see to it that in air strength and air power this country shall no longer be in a position inferior to any country within striking distance of our shores.<sup id="cite_ref-Middlemas2_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Middlemas2-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>On 29 March 1934 Germany published its defence estimates, which showed a total increase of one-third and an increase of 250% in its air force.<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A series of by-elections in late 1933 and early 1934 with massive swings against government candidates—most famous was <a href="/wiki/1933_Fulham_East_by-election" title="1933 Fulham East by-election">Fulham East</a> with a 26.5% swing— convinced Baldwin that the British public was profoundly pacifist.<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> Baldwin also rejected the "belligerent" views of those like Churchill and <a href="/wiki/Robert_Vansittart,_1st_Baron_Vansittart" title="Robert Vansittart, 1st Baron Vansittart">Robert Vansittart</a> because he believed that the Nazis were rational men who would appreciate the logic of mutual and equal deterrence.<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> He also believed war to be "the most fearful terror and prostitution of man's knowledge that ever was known".<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Prime_minister:_third_term_.281935.E2.80.931937.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Prime_minister:_third_term_(1935–1937)">Prime minister: third term (1935–1937)<span class="anchor" id="Third_premiership"></span></span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Stanley_Baldwin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Prime minister: third term (1935–1937)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Nation_Government_and_appointment">Nation Government and appointment</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Stanley_Baldwin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Nation Government and appointment">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/National_Government_(1935%E2%80%931937)" title="National Government (1935–1937)">National Government (1935–1937)</a></div>
<p>With MacDonald's health in decline, he and Baldwin changed places in June 1935: Baldwin was now prime minister, MacDonald Lord President of the council.<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> In October that year, Baldwin called a <a href="/wiki/1935_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1935 United Kingdom general election">general election</a>. Neville Chamberlain advised Baldwin to make rearmament the leading issue in the election campaign against Labour and said that if a rearmament programme was not announced until after the election, his government would be seen as having deceived the people.<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> However, Baldwin did not make rearmament the central issue in the election. He said that he would support the League of Nations, modernise Britain's defences and remedy deficiencies, but he also said: "I give you my word that there will be no great armaments".<sup id="cite_ref-Taylor1_56-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Taylor1-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> The main issues in the election were housing, unemployment and the special areas of economic depression.<sup id="cite_ref-Taylor1_56-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Taylor1-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> The election gave 430 seats to National Government supporters (386 of these Conservative) and 154 seats to Labour.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Rearmament">Rearmament</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Stanley_Baldwin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Rearmament">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Baldwin's younger son A. Windham Baldwin, writing in 1955, argued that his father, Stanley, had planned a rearmament programme as early as 1934 but had to do so quietly to avoid antagonising the public, whose pacifism was revealed by the <a href="/wiki/Peace_Ballot" title="Peace Ballot">Peace Ballot</a> of 1934–35 and endorsed by both the Labour and the Liberal oppositions. His thorough presentation of the case for rearmament in 1935, his son argued, defeated pacifism and secured a victory that allowed rearmament to move ahead.<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 31 July 1934, the Cabinet approved a report that called for expansion of the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Air_Force" title="Royal Air Force">Royal Air Force</a> to the 1923 standard by creating 40 new squadrons over the next five years.<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> On 26 November 1934, six days after receiving the news that the German air force would be as large as the RAF within one year, the Cabinet decided to speed up air rearmament from four years to two.<sup id="cite_ref-Barnett1_59-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Barnett1-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> On 28 November 1934, Churchill moved an amendment to the vote of thanks for the King's Speech: "the strength of our national defences, and especially our air defences, is no longer adequate".<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> His motion was known eight days before it was moved, and a special Cabinet meeting decided how to deal with the motion, which dominated two other Cabinet meetings.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> Churchill said Germany was rearming and requested that the money spent on air armaments be doubled or tripled to deter an attack and that the <i>Luftwaffe</i> was nearing equality with the RAF.<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> Baldwin responded by denying that the Luftwaffe was approaching equality and said it was "not 50 per cent" of the RAF. He added that by the end of 1935 the RAF would still have "a margin of nearly 50 per cent" in Europe.<sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> After Baldwin said that the government would ensure the RAF had parity with the future German air force, Churchill withdrew his amendment. In April 1935, the Air Secretary reported that although Britain's strength in the air would be ahead of Germany's for at least three years, air rearmament needed to be increased; so the Cabinet agreed to the creation of an extra 39 squadrons for home defence by 1937.<sup id="cite_ref-Barnett1_59-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Barnett1-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> However, on 8 May 1935, the Cabinet heard that it was estimated that the RAF was inferior to the <a href="/wiki/Luftwaffe" title="Luftwaffe">Luftwaffe</a> by 370 aircraft and that to reach parity, the RAF must have 3,800 aircraft by April 1937, an extra 1,400 above the existing air programme. It was learnt that Germany was easily able to outbuild that revised programme as well.<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> On 21 May 1935, the Cabinet agreed to expanding the home defence force of the RAF to 1,512 aircraft (840 bombers and 420 fighters).<sup id="cite_ref-Barnett1_59-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Barnett1-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> On 22 May 1935 Baldwin confessed in the Commons, "I was wrong in my estimate of the future. There I was completely wrong."<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 25 February 1936, the Cabinet approved a report calling for expansion of the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Navy" title="Royal Navy">Royal Navy</a> and the re-equipment of the <a href="/wiki/British_Army" title="British Army">British Army</a> (though not its expansion), along with the creation of "shadow factories" built by public money and managed by industrial companies. The factories came into operation in 1937. In February 1937, the Chiefs of Staff reported that by May 1937, the Luftwaffe would have 800 bombers, compared to the RAF's 48.<sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the debate in the Commons on 12 November 1936, Churchill attacked the government on rearmament as being "decided only to be undecided, resolved to be irresolute, adamant for drift, solid for fluidity, all-powerful to be impotent. So we go on, preparing more months and years – precious, perhaps vital, to the greatness of Britain – for the locusts to eat". Baldwin replied:
</p>
<blockquote><p>I put before the whole House my own views with an appalling frankness. From 1933, I and my friends were all very worried about what was happening in Europe. You will remember at that time the Disarmament Conference was sitting in Geneva. You will remember at that time there was probably a stronger pacifist feeling running through the country than at any time since the War. I am speaking of 1933 and 1934. You will remember the election at Fulham in the autumn of 1933.... That was the feeling of the country in 1933. My position as a leader of a great party was not altogether a comfortable one. I asked myself what chance was there... within the next year or two of that feeling being so changed that the country would give a mandate for rearmament? Supposing I had gone to the country and said that Germany was rearming and we must rearm, does anybody think that this pacific democracy would have rallied to that cry at that moment! I cannot think of anything that would have made the loss of the election from my point of view more certain.... We got from the country – with a large majority – a mandate for doing a thing that no one, twelve months before, would have believed possible.<sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Churchill wrote to a friend: "I have never heard such a squalid confession from a public man as Baldwin offered us yesterday".<sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup> In 1935 Baldwin wrote to <a href="/wiki/J._C._C._Davidson,_1st_Viscount_Davidson" class="mw-redirect" title="J. C. C. Davidson, 1st Viscount Davidson">J. C. C. Davidson</a> in a letter now lost that said of Churchill: "If there is going to be a war – and no one can say that there is not – we must keep him fresh to be our war Prime Minister".<sup id="cite_ref-Middlemas3_69-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Middlemas3-69">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Dugdale,_1st_Baron_Crathorne" title="Thomas Dugdale, 1st Baron Crathorne">Thomas Dugdale</a> also claimed Baldwin said to him: "If we do have a war, Winston must be Prime Minister. If he is in [the Cabinet] now we shan't be able to engage in that war as a united nation".<sup id="cite_ref-Middlemas3_69-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Middlemas3-69">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup> The General Secretary of the <a href="/wiki/Trades_Union_Congress" title="Trades Union Congress">Trades Union Congress</a>, <a href="/wiki/Walter_Citrine,_1st_Baron_Citrine" title="Walter Citrine, 1st Baron Citrine">Walter Citrine</a>, recalled a conversation he had had with Baldwin on 5 April 1943: "Baldwin thought his [Churchill's] political recovery was marvellous. He, personally, had always thought that if war came Winston would be the right man for the job".<sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Labour_Party_(UK)" title="Labour Party (UK)">Labour Party</a> strongly opposed the rearmament programme. <a href="/wiki/Clement_Attlee" title="Clement Attlee">Clement Attlee</a> said on 21 December 1933: "For our part, we are unalterably opposed to anything in the nature of rearmament".<sup id="cite_ref-Barnett2_71-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Barnett2-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> On 8 March 1934, Attlee said, after Baldwin defended the Air Estimates, "we on our side are out for total disarmament".<sup id="cite_ref-Middlemas2_49-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Middlemas2-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> On 30 July 1934, Labour moved a motion of censure against the government because of its planned expansion of the RAF. Attlee spoke for it: "We deny the need for increased air arms...and we reject altogether the claim of parity".<sup id="cite_ref-Barnett2_71-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Barnett2-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> Sir <a href="/wiki/Stafford_Cripps" title="Stafford Cripps">Stafford Cripps</a> also said on that occasion that it was fallacy that Britain could achieve security through increasing air armaments.<sup id="cite_ref-Barnett2_71-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Barnett2-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> On 22 May 1935, the day after Hitler had made a speech claiming that German rearmament offered no threat to peace, Attlee asserted that Hitler's speech gave "a chance to call a halt in the armaments race".<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> Attlee also denounced the Defence White Paper of 1937: "I do not believe the Government are going to get any safety through these armaments".<sup id="cite_ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Abdication_of_Edward_VIII">Abdication of Edward VIII</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Stanley_Baldwin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Abdication of Edward VIII">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The accession of King <a href="/wiki/Edward_VIII" title="Edward VIII">Edward VIII</a>, and the ensuing <a href="/wiki/Edward_VIII_abdication_crisis" class="mw-redirect" title="Edward VIII abdication crisis">abdication crisis</a>, brought Baldwin's last major test in office. The new monarch was "an ardent exponent of the cause of <a href="/wiki/Anglo-German_relations" class="mw-redirect" title="Anglo-German relations">Anglo-German understanding</a>" and had "strong views on his right to intervene in affairs of state," but the "Government's main fears... were of indiscretion."<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated1_74-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated1-74">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup> The King proposed to marry <a href="/wiki/Wallis_Simpson" title="Wallis Simpson">Wallis Simpson</a>, an American woman who was twice divorced. The high-minded Baldwin felt that he could tolerate her as "a respectable whore" as long as she stayed behind the throne but not as "Queen Wally".<sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Mrs. Simpson was also distrusted by the government for her known pro-German sympathies and was believed to be in "close contact with German monarchist circles".<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated1_74-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated1-74">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During October and November 1936, Baldwin joined the royal family in trying to dissuade the King from that marriage, arguing that the idea of having a twice-divorced woman as the Queen would be rejected by the government, by the country and by the Empire and that "the voice of the people must be heard."<sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> As the public standing of the King would be gravely compromised, the Prime Minister gave him time to reconsider the notion of this marriage.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated2_78-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated2-78">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> According to the historian Philip Williamson, "The offence lay in the implications of [the King's] attachment to Mrs. Simpson for the broader public morality and the constitutional integrity which were now perceived—especially by Baldwin—as underpinning the nation's unity and strength."<sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>News of the affair was broken in the newspapers on 2 December.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated3_80-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated3-80">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> There was some support for the wishes of the King, especially in and around London. The romantic royalists Churchill, <a href="/wiki/Oswald_Mosley" title="Oswald Mosley">Mosley</a>, and the press barons, <a href="/wiki/Max_Aitken,_1st_Baron_Beaverbrook" title="Max Aitken, 1st Baron Beaverbrook">Lord Beaverbrook</a> of the <i><a href="/wiki/Daily_Express" title="Daily Express">Daily Express</a></i> and <a href="/wiki/Harold_Harmsworth,_1st_Viscount_Rothermere" title="Harold Harmsworth, 1st Viscount Rothermere">Lord Rothermere</a> of the <i><a href="/wiki/Daily_Mail" title="Daily Mail">Daily Mail</a></i>, all declared that the king had a right to marry whichever woman he wished.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated3_80-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated3-80">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> The crisis assumed a political dimension when Beaverbrook and Churchill tried to rally support for the marriage in <a href="/wiki/Parliament_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="Parliament of the United Kingdom">Parliament</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Stuart_Ball_1-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Stuart_Ball-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> However, the King's party could muster only 40 <a href="/wiki/Members_of_Parliament" class="mw-redirect" title="Members of Parliament">Members of Parliament</a> in support,<sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> and the majority opinion sided with Baldwin and his Conservative government.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated3_80-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated3-80">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Labour_Party_(UK)" title="Labour Party (UK)">Labour</a> leader, <a href="/wiki/Clement_Attlee" title="Clement Attlee">Clement Attlee</a>, told Baldwin "that while Labour people had no objection to an American becoming Queen, [he] was certain they would not approve of Mrs. Simpson for that position", especially in the provinces and in the <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations" title="Commonwealth of Nations">Commonwealth countries</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Archbishop_of_Canterbury" title="Archbishop of Canterbury">Archbishop of Canterbury</a>, <a href="/wiki/Cosmo_Lang" class="mw-redirect" title="Cosmo Lang">Cosmo Lang</a>, held that the King, as the head of the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_England" title="Church of England">Church of England</a>, should not marry a divorcée.<sup id="cite_ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> <i><a href="/wiki/The_Times" title="The Times">The Times</a></i> argued that the monarchy's prestige would be destroyed if "private inclination were to come into open conflict with public duty and be allowed to prevail".<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated3_80-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated3-80">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>While some recent critics have complained that "Baldwin refused the reasonable request for time to reflect, preferring to keep the pressure on the King – once again suggesting that his own agenda was to force the crisis to a head" and that he "never mentioned that the alternative [to the marriage] was abdication",<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup> the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Commons_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="House of Commons of the United Kingdom">House of Commons</a> immediately and overwhelmingly came out against the marriage.<sup id="cite_ref-Stuart_Ball_1-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Stuart_Ball-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> The Labour and <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_(UK)" title="Liberal Party (UK)">Liberal</a> parties, the <a href="/wiki/Trades_Union_Congress" title="Trades Union Congress">Trades Union Congress</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated4_85-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated4-85">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> and the <a href="/wiki/Dominions" class="mw-redirect" title="Dominions">dominions</a> of Australia and Canada, all joined the <a href="/wiki/British_cabinet" class="mw-redirect" title="British cabinet">British cabinet</a> in rejecting the King's compromise, initially supported and perhaps conceived by<sup id="cite_ref-86" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-86">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup> Churchill,<sup id="cite_ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> for a <a href="/wiki/Morganatic_marriage" title="Morganatic marriage">morganatic marriage</a> that had originally been made on 16 November.<sup id="cite_ref-Stuart_Ball_1-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Stuart_Ball-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> The crisis threatened the unity of the <a href="/wiki/British_Empire" title="British Empire">British Empire</a>, since the King's personal relationship with the Dominions was their "only remaining constitutional link".<sup id="cite_ref-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-88">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Baldwin still hoped that the King would choose the throne over Mrs. Simpson.<sup id="cite_ref-Stuart_Ball_1-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Stuart_Ball-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> For the King to act against the wishes of the cabinet would have precipitated a <a href="/wiki/Constitutional_crisis" title="Constitutional crisis">constitutional crisis</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Stuart_Ball_1-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Stuart_Ball-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> Baldwin would have had to resign,<sup id="cite_ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup> and no other party leader would have served as the prime minister under the King,<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated2_78-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated2-78">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated3_80-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated3-80">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> with the Labour Party having already indicated that it would not form a ministry to uphold impropriety.<sup id="cite_ref-Stuart_Ball_1-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Stuart_Ball-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> Baldwin told the Cabinet, one Labour MP had asked, "Are we going to have a fascist monarchy?"<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated4_85-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated4-85">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> When the Cabinet refused the morganatic marriage, Edward decided to abdicate.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated3_80-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated3-80">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The King's final plea, on 4 December, to broadcast an appeal to the nation was rejected by the Prime Minister as too divisive.<sup id="cite_ref-Stuart_Ball_1-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Stuart_Ball-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-90">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup> Nevertheless, at his final audience with King Edward on 7 December, Baldwin offered to strive all night with the King's conscience, but he found Edward to be determined to go.<sup id="cite_ref-Stuart_Ball_1-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Stuart_Ball-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> Baldwin announced the King's abdication in the Commons on 10 December. <a href="/wiki/Harold_Nicolson" title="Harold Nicolson">Harold Nicolson</a>, an MP who witnessed Baldwin's speech, wrote in his diary:
</p>
<blockquote><p>There is no moment when he overstates emotion or indulges in oratory. There is intense silence broken only by the reporters in the gallery scuttling away to telephone the speech.... When it was over... [we] file out broken in body and soul, conscious that we have heard the best speech that we shall ever hear in our lives. There was no question of applause. It was the silence of Gettysburg...No man has ever dominated the House as he dominated it tonight, and he knows it.<sup id="cite_ref-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-91">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>After the speech, the House adjourned and Nicolson bumped into Baldwin as he was leaving, who asked him what he thought of the speech. Nicolson said it was superb to which Baldwin replied: "Yes&#160;... it was a success. I know it. It was almost wholly unprepared. I had a success, my dear Nicolson, at the moment I most needed it. Now is the time to go".<sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The King abdicated on 11 December and was succeeded by his brother, <a href="/wiki/George_VI_of_the_United_Kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="George VI of the United Kingdom">George VI</a>. Edward VIII was assigned the title of the <a href="/wiki/Duke_of_Windsor" title="Duke of Windsor">Duke of Windsor</a> by his brother and then married Mrs. Simpson in France in June 1937 after her divorce from <a href="/wiki/Ernest_Simpson" title="Ernest Simpson">Ernest Simpson</a> had become final.
</p><p>Baldwin had defused a political crisis by turning it into a constitutional question.<sup id="cite_ref-Stuart_Ball_1-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Stuart_Ball-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> His discreet resolution met with general approval and restored his popularity.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated3_80-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated3-80">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> He was praised on all sides for his tact and patience<sup id="cite_ref-Stuart_Ball_1-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Stuart_Ball-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> and was not in the least put out by the protestors' cries of "God save the King—from Baldwin!" "Flog Baldwin! Flog him!! We—want—Edward."<sup id="cite_ref-93" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-93">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/John_Charmley" title="John Charmley">John Charmley</a> argued in his history of the Conservative Party that Baldwin was pushing for more democracy and less of an old aristocratic upper-class tone. Monarchy was to be a national foundation by which the head of the Church, the State, and the Empire would draw upon 1000 years of tradition and could unify the nation. George V was an ideal fit: "an ordinary little man with the philistine tastes of most of his subjects, he could be presented as the archetypical English paterfamilias getting on with his duties without fuss." Charmley finds that George V and Baldwin, "made a formidable conservative team, with their ordinary, honest, English decency proving the first (and most effective) bulwark against revolution". Edward VIII, flaunting his upper-class playboy style, suffered from an unstable neurotic character and needed a strong stabilising partner, a role that Mrs. Simpson was unable to provide. Baldwin's final achievement was to smooth the way for Edward to abdicate in favour of his younger brother, who became George VI. Both father and son demonstrated the value of a democratic king during the severe physical and psychological hardships of the world wars, and the tradition was carried on by <a href="/wiki/Elizabeth_II" title="Elizabeth II">Elizabeth II</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-94" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-94">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Retirement">Retirement</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Stanley_Baldwin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Retirement">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Stanley_Baldwin,_wife_and_daughter_LCCN2014715581.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d0/Stanley_Baldwin%2C_wife_and_daughter_LCCN2014715581.jpg/220px-Stanley_Baldwin%2C_wife_and_daughter_LCCN2014715581.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="150" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d0/Stanley_Baldwin%2C_wife_and_daughter_LCCN2014715581.jpg/330px-Stanley_Baldwin%2C_wife_and_daughter_LCCN2014715581.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d0/Stanley_Baldwin%2C_wife_and_daughter_LCCN2014715581.jpg/440px-Stanley_Baldwin%2C_wife_and_daughter_LCCN2014715581.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4259" data-file-height="2908" /></a><figcaption>Baldwin photographed by the American press on board a ship, with his wife and daughter</figcaption></figure>

</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1063604349">.mw-parser-output .navbar{display:inline;font-size:88%;font-weight:normal}.mw-parser-output .navbar-collapse{float:left;text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .navbar-boxtext{word-spacing:0}.mw-parser-output .navbar ul{display:inline-block;white-space:nowrap;line-height:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::before{margin-right:-0.125em;content:"[ "}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::after{margin-left:-0.125em;content:" ]"}.mw-parser-output .navbar li{word-spacing:-0.125em}.mw-parser-output .navbar a>span,.mw-parser-output .navbar a>abbr{text-decoration:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-mini abbr{font-variant:small-caps;border-bottom:none;text-decoration:none;cursor:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-full{font-size:114%;margin:0 7em}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-mini{font-size:114%;margin:0 4em}</style><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Toryism" title="Template:Toryism"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Toryism" title="Template talk:Toryism"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:Toryism&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Leaving_office_and_peerage">Leaving office and peerage</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Stanley_Baldwin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=24" title="Edit section: Leaving office and peerage">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>After the <a href="/wiki/Coronation_of_George_VI" class="mw-redirect" title="Coronation of George VI">coronation of George VI</a>, Baldwin announced on 27 May 1937 that he would resign the premiership the next day. His last act as prime minister was to raise the salaries of MPs from £400 a year to £600 and to give the leader of the Opposition a salary. That was the first rise in MPs' wages since their introduction in 1911, and it particularly benefited Labour MPs. <a href="/wiki/Harold_Nicolson" title="Harold Nicolson">Harold Nicolson</a> wrote in his diary that it "was done with Baldwin's usual consummate taste. No man has ever left in such a blaze of affection".<sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> Baldwin was knighted as a <a href="/wiki/Knight_of_the_Garter" class="mw-redirect" title="Knight of the Garter">knight of the Garter</a> (KG) on 28 May<sup id="cite_ref-96" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-96">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup> and ennobled as <b><a href="/wiki/Earl_Baldwin_of_Bewdley" title="Earl Baldwin of Bewdley">Earl Baldwin of Bewdley</a></b> and <b>Viscount Corvedale</b>, <i>of <a href="/wiki/Corvedale" class="mw-redirect" title="Corvedale">Corvedale</a> in the County of Salop</i> on 8 June.<sup id="cite_ref-Stuart_Ball_1-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Stuart_Ball-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-97" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-97">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup> In a BBC radio broadcast transmitted on 8 December 1938, Baldwin made a nationwide appeal for funds to  help Jewish and other refugees fleeing persecution in Nazi Germany.<sup id="cite_ref-98" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-98">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup>  For this, Baldwin was dubbed a "guttersnipe" by a Berlin newspaper.<sup id="cite_ref-99" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-99">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup> The "Lord Baldwin Fund for Refugees", helping the kindertransport and other relief schemes, raised over £500,000 [£36,000,000 in 2022] <sup id="cite_ref-100" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-100">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Attitude_to_appeasement">Attitude to appeasement</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Stanley_Baldwin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=25" title="Edit section: Attitude to appeasement">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Baldwin supported the <a href="/wiki/Munich_Agreement" title="Munich Agreement">Munich Agreement</a> and said to Chamberlain on 26 September 1938: "If you can secure peace, you may be cursed by a lot of hotheads but my word you will be blessed in Europe and by future generations".<sup id="cite_ref-101" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-101">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup> Baldwin made a rare speech in the House of Lords on 4 October and said that he could not have gone to Munich but praised Chamberlain's courage. He also said the responsibility of a prime minister was not to commit a country to war until he was sure that it was ready to fight. If there was a 95% chance of war in the future, he would still choose peace. He also said he would put industry on a war footing the next day, as the opposition to such a move had disappeared.<sup id="cite_ref-102" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-102">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup> Churchill said in a speech: "He says he would mobilise tomorrow. I think it would have been much better if Earl Baldwin had said that two and a half years ago when everyone demanded a Ministry of Supply".<sup id="cite_ref-103" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-103">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Two weeks after Munich, Baldwin said prophetically in a conversation with <a href="/wiki/Victor_Montagu" title="Victor Montagu">Lord Hinchingbrooke</a>: "Can't we turn Hitler East? <a href="/wiki/Napoleon" title="Napoleon">Napoleon</a> <a href="/wiki/French_invasion_of_Russia" title="French invasion of Russia">broke himself against the Russians</a>. Hitler might <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Front_(World_War_II)" title="Eastern Front (World War II)">do the same</a>".<sup id="cite_ref-104" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-104">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Baldwin's years in retirement were quiet. After Chamberlain's death in 1940, Baldwin's perceived part in prewar <a href="/wiki/Appeasement" title="Appeasement">appeasement</a> made him an unpopular figure during and after <a href="/wiki/World_War_II" title="World War II">World War II</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Middlemas4_105-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Middlemas4-105">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> With a succession of British military failures in 1940, Baldwin started to receive critical letters: "insidious to begin with, then increasingly violent and abusive; then the newspapers; finally the polemicists who, with time and wit at their disposal, could debate at leisure how to wound the deepest".<sup id="cite_ref-Middlemas4_105-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Middlemas4-105">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> He did not have a secretary and so was not shielded from the often-unpleasant letters that were sent to him.<sup id="cite_ref-106" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-106">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup> After a bitterly-critical letter was sent to him by a member of the public, Baldwin wrote: "I can understand his bitterness. He wants a scapegoat and the men provided him with one". His biographers Middlemas and Barnes claim that "the men" almost certainly meant the authors of <i><a href="/wiki/Guilty_Men" title="Guilty Men">Guilty Men</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-107" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-107">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Letter_to_Lord_Halifax">Letter to Lord Halifax</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Stanley_Baldwin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=26" title="Edit section: Letter to Lord Halifax">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>After Lord Halifax made a speech on the strength of prayer as the instrument that could be invoked by the humblest to use in their country's service, Baldwin wrote to him on 23 July 1940:
</p>
<blockquote><p>With millions of others I had prayed hard at the time of <a href="/wiki/Dunkirk_evacuation" title="Dunkirk evacuation">Dunkirk</a> and never did prayer seem to be more speedily answered to the full. And we prayed for France and the next day she surrendered. I thought much, and when I went to bed I lay for a long time vividly awake. And I went over in my mind what had happened, concentrating on the thoughts that you had dwelt on, that prayer to be effective must be in accordance with God's will, and that by far the hardest thing to say from the heart and indeed the last lesson we learn (if we ever do) is to say and mean it, 'Thy will be done.' And I thought what mites we all are and how we can never see God's plan, a plan on such a scale that it <i>must</i> be incomprehensible. And suddenly for what must have been a couple of minutes I seemed to see with extraordinary and vivid clarity and to hear someone speaking to me. The words at the time were clear, but the recollection of them had passed when I seemed to come to, as it were, but the sense remained, and the sense was this. 'You cannot see the plan'; then 'Have you not thought there is a purpose in stripping you one by one of all the human props on which you depend, that you are being left alone in the world? You have now one upon whom to lean and I have chosen you as my instrument to work with my will. Why then are you afraid?' And to prove ourselves worthy of that tremendous task is our job.<sup id="cite_ref-108" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-108">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Iron_gates_criticism">Iron gates criticism</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Stanley_Baldwin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=27" title="Edit section: Iron gates criticism">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In September 1941, Baldwin's old enemy, Lord Beaverbrook, asked all local authorities to survey their area's iron and steel railings and gates that could be used for the war effort. Owners of such materials could appeal for an exemption on grounds of artistic or historic merit, which would be decided by a panel set up by local authorities. Baldwin applied for exemption for the iron gates of his country home on artistic grounds and his local council sent an architect to assess them. In December, the architect advised for them to be exempt, but in February 1942, the Ministry of Supply overruled that and said all his gates must go except the ones at the main entrance.<sup id="cite_ref-109" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-109">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup> A newspaper campaign hounded him for not donating the gates to war production. The <i><a href="/wiki/Daily_Mirror" title="Daily Mirror">Daily Mirror</a></i> columnist <i><a href="/wiki/William_Connor" title="William Connor">Cassandra</a></i> denounced Baldwin:
</p>
<blockquote><p>Here was the country in deadly peril with half the Empire swinging in the wind like a busted barn door hanging on one hinge. Here was Old England half smothered in a shroud crying for steel to cut her way out, and right in the heart of beautiful Worcestershire was a one-time Prime Minister, refusing to give up the gates of his estate to make guns for our defence – and his. Here was an old stupid politician who had tricked the nation into complacency about rearmament for fear of losing an election.... Here is the very shrine of stupidity.... This National Park of Failure....<sup id="cite_ref-110" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-110">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>There were fears that if the gates were not taken by the proper authorities, "others without authority might".<sup id="cite_ref-111" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-111">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup> Thus, months before any other collections were made, Baldwin's gates were removed except for those at the main entrance. Two of Beaverbrook's friends after the war claimed that it was Beaverbrook's decision despite Churchill saying, "Lay off Baldwin's gates".<sup id="cite_ref-112" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-112">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup> At <a href="/wiki/Question_Time" class="mw-redirect" title="Question Time">Question Time</a> in the House of Commons, Conservative MP Captain <a href="/wiki/Alan_Crosland_Graham" class="mw-redirect" title="Alan Crosland Graham">Alan Graham</a> said: "Is the honourable Member aware that it is very necessary to leave Lord Baldwin his gates in order to protect him from the just indignation of the mob?"<sup id="cite_ref-113" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-113">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Comments_on_politics">Comments on politics</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Stanley_Baldwin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=28" title="Edit section: Comments on politics">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>During the war, Churchill consulted him only once, in February 1943, on the advisability of his speaking out strongly against the continued neutrality of <a href="/wiki/%C3%89amon_de_Valera" title="Éamon de Valera">Éamon de Valera</a>'s <a href="/wiki/Republic_of_Ireland" title="Republic of Ireland">Ireland</a>. Baldwin saw the draft of Churchill's speech and advised against it, which Churchill followed.<sup id="cite_ref-114" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-114">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup> A few months after this visit to Churchill, Baldwin told Harold Nicolson, "I went into Downing Street.... a happy man. Of course it was partly because an old buffer like me enjoys feeling that he is still not quite out of things. But it was also pure patriotic joy that my country at such a time should have found such a leader. The furnace of the war has smeltered out all base metals from him".<sup id="cite_ref-115" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-115">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup> To D. H. Barber, Baldwin wrote of Churchill: "You can take it from me he is a really big man, the War has brought out the best that was in him. His head isn't turned the least little bit by the great position he occupies in the eyes of the world. I pray he is spared to see us through".<sup id="cite_ref-116" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-116">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In private, Baldwin defended his conduct in the 1930s:
</p>
<blockquote><p>the critics have no historical sense. I have no Cabinet papers by me and do not want to trust my memory. But recall the Fulham election, the peace ballot, Singapore, sanctions, Malta. The English will only learn by example. When I first heard of Hitler, when Ribbentrop came to see me, I thought they were all crazy. I think I brought Ramsay and Simon to meet Ribbentrop. Remember that Ramsay's health was breaking up in the last two years. He had lost his nerve in the House in the last year. I had to take all the important speeches. The moment he went, I prepared for a general election and got a bigger majority for rearmament. No power on earth could have got rearmament without a general election except by a big split. Simon was inefficient. I had to lead the House, keep the machine together with those Labour fellows.<sup id="cite_ref-Middlemas5_117-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Middlemas5-117">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>In December 1944, strongly advised by friends, Baldwin decided to respond to criticisms of him through a biographer. He asked <a href="/wiki/G._M._Young" title="G. M. Young">G. M. Young</a>, who accepted, and asked Churchill to grant permission to Young to see Cabinet papers. Baldwin wrote:
</p>
<blockquote><p>I am the last person to complain of fair criticism, but when one book after another appears and I am compared, for example, to <a href="/wiki/Pierre_Laval" title="Pierre Laval">Laval</a>, my gorge rises; but I am crippled and cannot go and examine the files of the Cabinet Office. Could G. M. Young go on my behalf?<sup id="cite_ref-Middlemas5_117-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Middlemas5-117">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Last_years_and_death">Last years and death</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Stanley_Baldwin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=29" title="Edit section: Last years and death">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Worcester_Cathedral_Baldwin_of_Bewdley_grave.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e6/Worcester_Cathedral_Baldwin_of_Bewdley_grave.jpg/220px-Worcester_Cathedral_Baldwin_of_Bewdley_grave.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="263" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e6/Worcester_Cathedral_Baldwin_of_Bewdley_grave.jpg/330px-Worcester_Cathedral_Baldwin_of_Bewdley_grave.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/e/e6/Worcester_Cathedral_Baldwin_of_Bewdley_grave.jpg 2x" data-file-width="362" data-file-height="432" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Worcester_Cathedral" title="Worcester Cathedral">Worcester Cathedral</a>, grave of the 1st Earl Baldwin of Bewdley and his wife <a href="/wiki/Lucy_Baldwin" title="Lucy Baldwin">Lucy, née Ridsdale</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>In June 1945, Baldwin's wife, <a href="/wiki/Lucy_Baldwin" title="Lucy Baldwin">Lucy</a>, died. Baldwin himself now suffered from <a href="/wiki/Arthritis" title="Arthritis">arthritis</a> and needed a cane to walk. When he made his final public appearance in London in October 1947 at the unveiling of a statue of <a href="/wiki/George_V" title="George V">George V</a>, a crowd of people recognised and cheered him, but he had become deaf and so asked: "Are they booing me?"<sup id="cite_ref-118" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-118">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup> Having been made <a href="/wiki/List_of_Chancellors_of_the_University_of_Cambridge" class="mw-redirect" title="List of Chancellors of the University of Cambridge">Chancellor of the University of Cambridge</a> in 1930, he continued in that capacity until his death in his sleep at <a href="/wiki/Astley_Hall_(Stourport-on-Severn)" class="mw-redirect" title="Astley Hall (Stourport-on-Severn)">Astley Hall</a>, near <a href="/wiki/Stourport-on-Severn" title="Stourport-on-Severn">Stourport-on-Severn</a>, <a href="/wiki/Worcestershire" title="Worcestershire">Worcestershire</a>, on 14 December 1947. He was cremated in Birmingham, and his ashes were buried in <a href="/wiki/Worcester_Cathedral" title="Worcester Cathedral">Worcester Cathedral</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Stuart_Ball_1-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Stuart_Ball-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> No cause of death was revealed.
</p><p>Baldwin was a member of the <a href="/wiki/Oddfellows" class="mw-redirect" title="Oddfellows">Oddfellows</a> and <a href="/wiki/Foresters_Friendly_Society" title="Foresters Friendly Society">Foresters Friendly Society</a>.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (September 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy">Legacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Stanley_Baldwin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=30" title="Edit section: Legacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Astley,_Worcs,_Baldwin_memorial_1.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/23/Astley%2C_Worcs%2C_Baldwin_memorial_1.jpg/220px-Astley%2C_Worcs%2C_Baldwin_memorial_1.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="180" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/23/Astley%2C_Worcs%2C_Baldwin_memorial_1.jpg/330px-Astley%2C_Worcs%2C_Baldwin_memorial_1.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/23/Astley%2C_Worcs%2C_Baldwin_memorial_1.jpg/440px-Astley%2C_Worcs%2C_Baldwin_memorial_1.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1712" data-file-height="1398" /></a><figcaption>Memorial to the 1st Earl Baldwin of Bewdley near his home, <a href="/wiki/Astley_Hall_(Stourport-on-Severn)" class="mw-redirect" title="Astley Hall (Stourport-on-Severn)">Astley Hall</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Upon his retirement in 1937, he had received a great deal of praise, but the onset of <a href="/wiki/World_War_II" title="World War II">World War II</a> would change his public image for the worse. Baldwin, Chamberlain and MacDonald were held responsible for Great Britain's military unpreparedness on the eve of war in 1939. <a href="/wiki/Peter_Howard_(journalist)" title="Peter Howard (journalist)">Peter Howard</a>, writing in the <i>Sunday Express</i> (3 September 1939), accused Baldwin of deceiving the country of the dangers that faced it in order not to rearm and so win the 1935 general election.<sup id="cite_ref-119" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-119">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup> During the ill-fated <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_France" title="Battle of France">Battle of France</a> in May 1940, Lloyd George in conversation with Churchill and <a href="/wiki/Edmund_Ironside,_1st_Baron_Ironside" title="Edmund Ironside, 1st Baron Ironside">General Ironside</a> railed against Baldwin and said that "he ought to be hanged".<sup id="cite_ref-120" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-120">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In July 1940, a bestseller <i><a href="/wiki/Guilty_Men" title="Guilty Men">Guilty Men</a></i> appeared, which blamed Baldwin for failing to rearm enough. In May 1941, <a href="/wiki/Hamilton_Fyfe" title="Hamilton Fyfe">Hamilton Fyfe</a> wrote an article ("Leadership and Democracy") for <i>Nineteenth Century and After</i>, which also laid those charges against Baldwin. In 1941, <a href="/wiki/A._L._Rowse" title="A. L. Rowse">A. L. Rowse</a> criticised Baldwin for lulling the people into a false sense of security and as a practitioner in "the art of taking the people in":
</p>
<blockquote><p>what can this man think in the still watches of the night, when he contemplates the ordeal his country is going through as the result of the years, the locust years, in which he held power?<sup id="cite_ref-121" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-121">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Churchill firmly believed that Baldwin's conciliatory stance toward Hitler gave the impression that in the case of an attack by the German dictator, Britain would not fight. Churchill was known for his magnanimity toward political rivals such as Chamberlain but had none to spare for Baldwin. "I wish Stanley Baldwin no ill," Churchill said in declining to send him 80th birthday greetings in 1947, "but it would have been much better had he never lived." Churchill also believed that Baldwin, rather than Chamberlain, would be most blamed by subsequent generations for the policies that led to "the most unnecessary war in history".  An index entry in the first volume of Churchill's "History of the Second World War" (<i>The Gathering Storm</i>) records Baldwin "admitting to putting party before country" for his alleged admission that he would not have won the 1935 election if he had pursued a more aggressive policy of rearmament. Churchill selectively quoted a speech in the Commons by Baldwin that gave the false impression that Baldwin was speaking of the general election, instead of the Fulham by-election in 1933, and omitted Baldwin's actual comments about the 1935 election: "We got from the country, a mandate for doing a thing [a substantial rearmament programme] that no one, twelve months before, would have believed possible".<sup id="cite_ref-122" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-122">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup> In his speech on Baldwin's death, Churchill paid him a double-edged yet respectful tribute: "He was the most formidable politician I ever encountered in public life".<sup id="cite_ref-123" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-123">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1948, <a href="/wiki/Reginald_Bassett" title="Reginald Bassett">Reginald Bassett</a> published an essay disputing the claim that Baldwin "confessed" to putting party before country and claimed that Baldwin was referring to 1933 and 1934 when a general election on rearmament would have been lost.<sup id="cite_ref-124" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-124">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1952, <a href="/wiki/G._M._Young" title="G. M. Young">G. M. Young</a> published an authorised biography of Baldwin that asserted that Baldwin united the nation and helped moderate the policies of the Labour Party. However, Young accepted the chief criticisms of Baldwin that he failed to rearm early enough and that he put party before country. Young contends that Baldwin should have retired in 1935. Churchill and Beaverbrook deemed several passages in the biography to be defamatory of their own actions and threatened to sue if they were not removed or altered. A settlement was reached to remove the offending sentences, and the publisher <a href="/wiki/Rupert_Hart-Davis" title="Rupert Hart-Davis">Rupert Hart-Davis</a> had the "hideously expensive" job of removing and replacing seven leaves from 7,580 copies.<sup id="cite_ref-125" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-125">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In response to Young's biography, <a href="/wiki/D._C._Somervell" title="D. C. Somervell">D. C. Somervell</a> published <i>Stanley Baldwin: An examination of some features of Mr. G. M. Young's biography</i> in 1953 with a foreword by <a href="/wiki/Ernest_Brown_(MP)" class="mw-redirect" title="Ernest Brown (MP)">Ernest Brown</a>. This attempted to defend Baldwin against the charges made by Young. Both Young and Somervell were criticised by <a href="/wiki/C._L._Mowat" title="C. L. Mowat">C. L. Mowat</a> in 1955, who claimed that they both failed to rehabilitate Baldwin's reputation.<sup id="cite_ref-126" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-126">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1126788409"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1045330069"><table class="sidebar sidebar-collapse nomobile nowraplinks" style="width:22.0em; border: 4px double #0000CD; background:#FFFFFF;"><tbody><tr><td class="sidebar-pretitle">Part of a <a href="/wiki/Category:Conservatism" title="Category:Conservatism">series</a> on</td></tr><tr><th class="sidebar-title-with-pretitle"><b><a href="/wiki/Conservatism" title="Conservatism">Conservatism</a></b></th></tr><tr><td class="sidebar-image" style="padding-top:0.6em;"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/Political_colour#Blue" title="Political colour"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/17/Blue_flag_waving.svg/75px-Blue_flag_waving.svg.png" decoding="async" width="75" height="81" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/17/Blue_flag_waving.svg/113px-Blue_flag_waving.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/17/Blue_flag_waving.svg/150px-Blue_flag_waving.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="249" data-file-height="268" /></a></span></td></tr><tr><td class="sidebar-content">
